14:I[8669,["/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/08cykig6vtx_p.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/07qi2okp-.yp9.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0frujd.eyhz4o.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0n4z.7yu76je-.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/09-o.77-p0p36.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/1304s0wcp2kce.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0j1uy9t~zxcf0.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/14_5o-nbg8k_r.js"],"DateSidebar"]
15:I[44692,["/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/08cykig6vtx_p.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/07qi2okp-.yp9.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0frujd.eyhz4o.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0n4z.7yu76je-.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/09-o.77-p0p36.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/1304s0wcp2kce.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0j1uy9t~zxcf0.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/14_5o-nbg8k_r.js"],"DailyView"]
16:T8c4,The paper starts by separating recursive training into three paradigms: replace, accumulate, and selection. Replace uses only newly generated synthetic data and is already known to collapse quickly. Accumulate keeps the original real data and appends later generations, which can prevent variance blow-up in theory, but in low-resource settings the real seed set is often too small to dominate later synthetic rounds. The main focus is selection: generate a candidate synthetic pool, score it with a verifier, and keep only the “good” subset for the next round.

The key move is to model the verifier’s reference distribution explicitly as a biased local observation rather than an approximation to the global truth. Under this view, the selection operator is not a denoiser. It is a directional projection toward the local manifold. Samples are retained in proportion to how well they match the silo’s own support, so repeated selection trims away globally important but locally underrepresented regions. The paper argues that this is not a transient artifact. In recursive training, the bias compounds and drives diversity contraction at an asymptotic power-law rate, with worsening Wasserstein distance to the true global distribution.

To mitigate this, the authors propose a collaborative verifier framework under a no-raw-data-sharing constraint. Instead of pooled verification, they build proxy reference distributions across silos using Wasserstein geometry. Two constructions are emphasized: geodesic interpolations between local empirical distributions, and Wasserstein barycenters across multiple silos. These proxies are then used as collective references for scoring synthetic samples. The design choice is important. Simple feature averaging, parameter averaging, or majority voting would not preserve distributional geometry well enough, especially for tail coverage and mode structure. Wasserstein proxies are intended to recover a more faithful global reference at the distribution level while respecting silo boundaries. The paper also notes that access to the true global distribution would theoretically fix the problem, but that option is unavailable in the target regime, which is why collaborative proxies matter.17:T6d2,The experiments are designed to validate mechanism rather than to chase a single downstream score. Section 6 is the main place to read. The setup simulates low-resource recursive training with multiple silos and compares no selection, local selection, global-reference selection, and collaborative selection based on geodesic or barycenter proxies.

The main findings are consistent with the theory. Local selection can improve short-term alignment with the silo’s own data, but it accelerates global diversity loss: variance contracts, tail modes disappear, and Wasserstein distance to the true distribution grows. This degradation compounds across generations rather than appearing as a one-off effect, matching the paper’s power-law collapse analysis. When selection is performed against a global reference, collapse is much weaker, which isolates the real issue: not selection per se, but insufficient reference coverage. Under the realistic no-sharing constraint, both Wasserstein geodesic and barycenter proxies outperform single-silo selection, with larger gains when silo heterogeneity is stronger.

The abstract and the provided full-text excerpt do not expose all numeric tables, so it would be wrong to invent exact percentages or dataset sizes here. The right way to read the results is to inspect the main tables and generation-wise curves in Section 6: Wasserstein distance, variance evolution, diversity coverage, and ablations over silo count, resource level, and heterogeneity. The most informative signal is the trajectory shape over recursive rounds. Local verification often looks acceptable early on, then collapses faster later; collaborative proxies mainly help by preserving tails and improving long-horizon stability.18:T797,Read it in four passes: setup, mechanism, mitigation, validation.

Start with the Introduction and Preliminaries. The important point is not the generic model-collapse background, but the paper’s reframing: selection is only as good as the reference distribution behind the verifier. Keep the replace / accumulate / selection distinction clear, because the later claims depend on it.

Then read Section 3 carefully. This is the conceptual center of the paper. Focus on how the authors formalize local-prior-driven biased filtering, and why that bias becomes diversity contraction under recursion. Subsection 3.2 is especially important because it links the selection bias to asymptotic power-law collapse. Pay attention to which assumptions are doing the work: low-resource verification, fragmented support, and the monotonicity of the selection rule.

Next, read Section 4 on the Wasserstein collaborative framework. The key question is not the algebra itself, but why geodesic interpolation and barycenters are the right objects here, instead of simpler pooled statistics or ensemble voting. The interesting technical issue is what exactly the proxy restores: mean location, support coverage, tail mass, or all three.

Finally, read Section 6 with an eye on dynamics rather than endpoint scores. Prioritize the generation-wise plots for variance, Wasserstein distance, and diversity coverage. If time is limited, first compare local selection, collaborative selection, and global-reference selection; then look at the difference between geodesic and barycenter proxies.

Two follow-up questions are worth carrying forward. One is whether the same verifier-bias logic applies to judge models, reward models, and synthetic-data filters used in modern post-training loops. The other is whether distribution-level collaboration can be extended beyond raw data distributions to tokenization space, embedding space, or text-generation manifolds.19:T846,Gefen has two main components.

The first is shared second-moment estimation. The paper starts from a theoretical analysis on a two-layer MLP and studies when two parameters with a large mixed Hessian entry should also have similar squared-gradient magnitudes. The key result is that, under feasible Hessian bounds, increasing the magnitude of the mixed Hessian contracts the admissible ratio of squared gradients toward 1. This gives a clean design principle: parameters that are strongly coupled in curvature are natural candidates for sharing Adam’s second-moment estimate. Since explicit Hessian computation is intractable in large models, Gefen uses the structure of the first-step squared gradients as a proxy. For each parameter tensor, it flattens the tensor, enumerates proper divisors p of the tensor length n, reshapes g0^2 into consecutive blocks of size p, and computes an intra-block heterogeneity score E(p). Small E(p) means the squared gradients within each block are similar, so replacing element-wise v with one shared scalar per block should incur limited distortion. Larger p gives more aggressive sharing and lower memory, so the method selects p by balancing compression against heterogeneity. An important empirical observation is that E(p) is not necessarily monotonic in p; some larger divisors align better with real structural patterns such as attention heads or channel groups. That is why automatic search can outperform hand-written grouping rules.

The second component is first-moment compression. Instead of using a generic low-bit quantizer, Gefen reuses the same block partition and quantizes the first moment m with a learned codebook. The assignment is solved exactly with dynamic programming. Two design choices matter here. First, the codebook is learned rather than manually specified. Second, reusing the same partition avoids introducing another independent block-size hyperparameter. In effect, Gefen is a joint optimizer-state compression scheme: structured sharing for v and codebook-based quantization for m, both built around an automatically inferred block structure.1a:T6d7,The main empirical claims are threefold. First, memory: Gefen reduces optimizer-state memory by about 8× relative to AdamW, corresponding to roughly 6.5 GiB saved per 1B parameters. That is the headline systems result. Second, quality retention: across both LLM and non-LLM models, the method maintains performance comparable to AdamW, with some settings showing slight gains. The introduction explicitly states that Gefen achieves the lowest memory footprint among compared methods while keeping quality and throughput comparable or better. Third, the automatic grouping mechanism appears to capture real structure rather than arbitrary compression. Beyond the toy theory, the appendix shows similar behavior in real tensors, including attention and convolutional layers, where the discovered partitions produce more homogeneous squared gradients within blocks.

The most relevant baselines are AdamW, Adam-mini, and quantized optimizer variants. Adam-mini reduces memory through manually specified sharing patterns, but requires architecture information such as the number of attention heads and depends on tensor naming. Gefen removes that dependency. Quantized optimizers often rely on hand-crafted codebooks and an extra block-size hyperparameter; Gefen replaces those with a learned codebook and exact dynamic programming. Another practical point is overhead: the grouping pattern is inferred once from the first-step gradient rather than recomputed throughout training, so the method avoids a large recurring cost. When reading the experimental section, the most informative comparisons are final loss or perplexity gaps versus AdamW, throughput changes, and ablations for shared-v only, quantized-m only, and automatic versus manual grouping.1b:T5f0,Read it in three passes: theory, algorithm, then experiments.

Start with the theoretical section and focus on the central statement: large mixed-Hessian entries force the ratio of squared gradients toward 1. You do not need every proof detail on the first pass, but it is worth understanding the assumptions, the scope of the result, and why the authors argue that the same phenomenon appears beyond the toy MLP setting. This is the part that justifies block-wise sharing as more than an engineering shortcut.

Then move to the algorithm section. Three details deserve careful attention: why the first-step gradient g0 is sufficient to infer a useful sharing structure; how the heterogeneity metric E(p) is defined and used to trade off distortion against memory; and why first-moment quantization is tied to the same partition, with dynamic programming used for exact assignment. If you care about implementation and recipe design, this is the section to study closely.

For experiments, prioritize the main result tables, memory accounting, throughput comparisons, and ablations. The key question is not whether Gefen wins by a tiny metric margin, but whether it preserves AdamW-level optimization under an 8× state reduction, and whether it does so broadly across model families. The appendix visualizations of squared-gradient structure in attention and convolution tensors are also useful; they help assess whether the automatic partitioning is discovering meaningful structure or merely fitting first-step noise.1c:Ta8f,方法上，论文研究的是带动量的 normalised steepest descent。更新形式不是沿欧式梯度直接走，而是在给定范数下做单位步方向的最速下降；Muon 对应的几何是谱范数，因而其对偶平稳性指标是核范数。技术难点在于：在一般 Banach 空间里，重尾随机噪声的 martingale 控制不像 Hilbert 空间那样可以直接用内积工具，现有工作往往借助范数等价常数 ρ 把问题转回欧式空间，导致维度依赖。论文的关键推进有三点。

其一，直接在原生对偶范数下假设 heavy-tailed noise，即随机梯度误差满足 p 阶中心矩有界，而不是有界方差。这样做更贴近经验现象，也为 geometry-aware bound 留出空间。

其二，针对对偶范数 p-uniformly smooth 的情形，作者建立了带动量的非欧几里得归一化最速下降的最优样本复杂度：达到 ε-||·||_* 平稳点所需复杂度为 O(Δ_1 L / ε^2 · (σ/ε)^{p/(p-1)})。这里 Δ_1 是初始函数差距量级，L 是光滑常数，σ 描述重尾噪声强度。这个结果的重要之处不只是 rate 本身，而是它在更强的对偶范数平稳性下成立，并且不再通过 ρ 把噪声项粗暴地欧式化。论文还把分析扩展到较弱的 (L0, L1)-smoothness 假设，而不是只处理标准全局 L-smooth。

其三，Muon 的对偶核范数并不满足 uniformly smooth，因此不能直接套一般定理。作者为 Schatten-1 几何单独做了更细的分析，得到 Muon 在 m×n 矩阵上的样本复杂度大致为 O(min{m,n} · Δ_1 L / ε^2 · (σ/ε)^{p/(p-1)})，并指出这已经优于欧式算法在“校正到同样核范数平稳性指标”后的复杂度。直观上，欧式方法若想保证核范数意义下的 ε-stationarity，需要通过 Frobenius-to-nuclear 的范数转换支付更差的、且带 p 依赖的维度因子；Muon 则在原生几何里吸收了大部分重尾噪声代价。论文还给出针对 Schatten-1 平稳性的 first-order lower bound，量级与上界匹配到主要因子，说明 Muon 在这一问题类上是一阶最优，且不存在完全无维度代价的保证。

从方法区别看，和传统 SGD/Adam 理论相比，这篇工作不是再做一次 adaptive method 收敛证明，而是把“优化几何”“平稳性度量”“噪声尾部形状”三者绑在一起分析。和已有非欧式优化理论相比，它不再依赖 bounded variance + norm equivalence 的标准套路，而是引入函数分析工具直接控制一般范数下的噪声项。这部分工具本身也可能被后续用于分析其他矩阵几何优化器，如 Scion 或更一般的 Schatten-p 结构更新。1d:T45a,For pretraining research, the contribution matters for three reasons. First, it gives a theoretical explanation for a class of optimizers that has already appeared in serious Transformer training setups. This is not just a generic convergence proof; it explains when and why matrix-valued non-Euclidean updates help under heavy-tailed gradient noise. Second, it argues that the noise model should be expressed in the optimizer’s native geometry instead of being projected back to Euclidean space. That is a useful lens for large-scale training, where parameters are naturally matrices and gradient noise is often empirically heavy-tailed. Third, the paper provides near-optimal sample complexity for Muon under nuclear-norm stationarity, together with a matching lower bound showing that a fully dimension-free guarantee is impossible in that metric. That changes how one should read the remaining dimension factor: it is not merely a proof artifact. In scale, this is more than a minor recipe refinement; it is a principled justification for why a new optimizer family can be well matched to modern pretraining.1e:Tb63,The algorithmic object is momentum-based normalized steepest descent in a general normed space. Instead of taking a Euclidean gradient step, the method moves along the steepest descent direction normalized in the chosen geometry. Muon corresponds to using the spectral norm on matrices, whose dual stationarity measure is the Schatten-1, or nuclear, norm.

The technical difficulty is the stochastic term. In Hilbert spaces, martingale noise can be controlled with inner-product tools. In general Banach spaces, that route is unavailable, so prior work often falls back to norm equivalence, introducing a factor rho that can scale badly with dimension. This paper avoids that standard reduction.

The first key move is to formulate the heavy-tailed assumption directly in the native dual norm: stochastic gradient errors have bounded p-th central moments, rather than bounded variance. This is both more realistic and more geometry-aware.

The second contribution is a general convergence theorem for momentum normalized steepest descent when the dual norm is p-uniformly smooth. Under this condition, the method reaches an epsilon-stationary point in the dual norm with sample complexity O(Delta_1 L / epsilon^2 * (sigma/epsilon)^{p/(p-1)}). The result is notable because it holds for a stronger stationarity notion than the usual Euclidean one, and because the noise is handled directly in the native geometry. The analysis also extends to weak (L0, L1)-smoothness, not only standard global L-smoothness.

The third contribution is a Muon-specific analysis. The nuclear norm is not uniformly smooth, so the general theorem does not apply directly. The authors therefore derive a dedicated bound for Schatten-1 stationarity on m by n matrices, obtaining a complexity of the form O(min{m,n} * Delta_1 L / epsilon^2 * (sigma/epsilon)^{p/(p-1)}). They then compare this with Euclidean methods after correcting for the stronger stationarity metric. The message is that Euclidean algorithms can only match the same nuclear-norm stationarity by paying a worse, p-dependent dimension penalty, whereas Muon absorbs heavy-tailed noise much more naturally in its own geometry. The paper also proves a first-order lower bound for Schatten-1 stationarity, showing that Muon is first-order optimal up to the main factors and that a dimension-free guarantee cannot exist in this setting.

Relative to standard SGD or Adam theory, the novelty is not another adaptive-optimizer convergence proof. The paper jointly analyzes optimization geometry, stationarity metric, and tail behavior of stochastic gradients. Relative to earlier non-Euclidean analyses, it replaces the bounded-variance plus norm-equivalence template with functional-analytic tools that control noise directly under general norms. Those tools may be reusable beyond Muon, for other matrix-geometry optimizers such as Scion or related Schatten-structured methods.1f:T5d8,This is primarily a theory paper, so the main results are theorems, upper bounds, and lower bounds rather than large-scale pretraining runs. The most important quantitative takeaways are the complexity expressions.

First, under p-uniformly smooth dual geometry, the sample complexity for reaching epsilon-stationarity in the dual norm is O(Delta_1 L / epsilon^2 * (sigma/epsilon)^{p/(p-1)}). When p=2, this recovers the more familiar bounded-variance style behavior. When p<2, the heavy-tailed regime appears through the factor (sigma/epsilon)^{p/(p-1)}.

Second, for Muon on m by n matrices, the paper derives a nuclear-norm stationarity bound whose leading dimension term is min{m,n}. The important comparison is not against Euclidean Frobenius stationarity, but against Euclidean methods forced to guarantee the same Schatten-1 stationarity. Under that fair comparison, Euclidean methods pay a worse and p-dependent dimensional cost.

Third, the lower bound for Schatten-1 stationarity shows that a dimension-free guarantee is impossible. This matters because it means the remaining min{m,n} factor in Muon’s upper bound is not simply loose analysis.

If the full paper includes numerical illustrations, they are likely sanity checks on synthetic heavy-tailed settings rather than full GPT-scale training benchmarks. The right thing to extract is not downstream accuracy or token-level loss, but how the upper and lower bounds compare and how the geometry changes the stochastic complexity.20:T5bd,For a skim-level read, start with the introduction, then jump to the main theorem and the Muon-specific section. Around 30 to 45 minutes should be enough to capture the main argument; a proof-oriented read will take substantially longer.

Three parts deserve priority. First, the introduction, especially the discussion of why norm-equivalence-based analyses hide the benefit of non-Euclidean methods and why nuclear-norm stationarity is a stronger target than Frobenius-norm stationarity. Second, the general theorem for p-uniformly smooth dual norms: focus on the exact heavy-tailed assumption and the resulting complexity form. Third, the Muon/Schatten-1 section together with the lower bound, because that is where the paper answers the practical question: what is special about Muon, where does the gain come from, and why can the dimension factor not be removed entirely.

If time is limited, skip most of the functional-analysis details and retain two ideas: how the paper avoids Euclidean martingale reduction, and why the nuclear norm, despite not being uniformly smooth, still admits a dedicated analysis. If you work on optimizer theory, the noise-control tools are worth studying carefully. If you work on pretraining systems, the main value is the decision rule it suggests: when gradients are heavy-tailed, parameters are matrix-structured, and the relevant geometry is spectral or nuclear rather than Euclidean, Muon’s advantage has a principled basis.21:T4c9,NULLs partitions MLP neurons into shared backbone neurons and a pool of sparsely activated sinks. Each source identity is deterministically mapped to a sparse mask over the sink pool. During training, examples from that source activate the shared backbone plus the source’s masked sinks. The implementation is close to standard pretraining: the main extra operation is an elementwise mask over MLP activations. Unlike per-source modules, NULLs does not allocate a separate parameter block per source; sparse masks over a common sink pool provide combinatorial control, so the number of controllable sources can be much larger than the number of sink groups. The mechanism relies on training dynamics. A fact unique to one source produces gradients on both the backbone and that source’s sinks, but the backbone receives interfering updates from all other sources, while the relevant sinks see far less interference. The unique fact is therefore fitted by the sinks earlier. Once fitted there, the gradient pressure on the backbone decreases, reducing leakage into shared weights. Facts repeated across many sources keep reinforcing the backbone. At deployment, unlearning a source means disabling its corresponding sinks.22:T4ba,The paper presents two main case studies. In the Wikipedia setting, it trains an approximately 1B-parameter model on a corpus where about 6 million articles are treated as separate sources. The key test is single-article unlearning without broader topic erasure. Disabling the target article’s sinks sharply reduces recall of facts unique to that article, while preserving semantically related knowledge from other articles. Post-hoc unlearning baselines tend to remove related knowledge at a similar rate as the target knowledge. In the Harry Potter setting, the source is coarser and more topical. NULLs supports instantaneous removal by disabling the relevant sinks, and the removal is more robust to an adversarial relearning attack; the paper notes that standard gradient unlearning can be reversed in fewer than 10 gradient steps. On general natural-language benchmarks, NULLs matches a standard Transformer closely, suggesting that the sink mechanism does not impose a visible cost on broad capability. The most important experimental details to inspect are sink capacity, mask sparsity, source granularity, mask overlap, and the relationship between factual recall suppression and perplexity changes.23:T56d,The probe suite contains 686 prompts across six regimes. The strengthened run uses 5 seeds and 20 prompts per regime, giving 100 traces per regime, with prompt-clustered bootstrap confidence intervals. For prose, code, math, and factual regimes, token-level tie-aware Kendall tau_b is moderate, roughly 0.43 to 0.60. The ordering becomes more left-to-right as the analysis bin gets coarser; for example, in R1, tau_b rises from 0.59 at bin size 4 to 0.91 at bin size 64. There is no discontinuity at 16 tokens, so 16 is not supported as a privileged architectural block. The observed order remains below a pure block-sequential control, which is around 0.94 to 0.96, indicating real sub-block disorder. Accept batches are large, around 13 to 26 content tokens per call, so many token pairs are tied. The JSON regime is different: token tau_b is -0.044 with a 95% CI of [-0.086, -0.00], which is closer to order-independent behavior. Commit confidence is predictive on GSM8K, with AUROC 0.749 and a 95% CI of [0.602, 0.879], but not on factual recall, where AUROC is 0.471 with a 95% CI of [0.383, 0.544]. Generations typically finish in a small number of accept-calls, far below the 48-step budget, and at entropy well below the 0.1 bound. On scorable regimes, accuracy is comparable to the matched AR sibling Gemma-4 26B-A4B, although the comparison is not powered as an equivalence test.24:T4d0,The target DiT has 34 layers, hidden size 4608, 18 attention heads with head dimension 256, and FFN intermediate size 12288. With classifier-free guidance, each branch contains 211 linear layers. The five main GEMM shapes are qkv (13824, 4608), attention output (4608, 4608), ffn-up (12288, 4608), ffn-down (4608, 12288), and LLM projection (4608, 53248). The int8 × int8 to int32 accumulation is bit-exact against torch._int_mm, and the dequantized output matches the reference with cosine similarity 1.0 and no NaNs. On RTX 3090, individual GEMMs are 2.8–4.2× faster than bf16 and 4–8× faster than the unfused int8 path. End-to-end generation improves by about 9–10% at 768px. At 1024px, the fused path runs on a single RTX 3090 at 156.5 seconds per image, compared with previously reported 172.9 seconds for FP8, 164.5 seconds for NF4, and 184–185 seconds for fake-quant INT8. Quality appears unchanged on the reported PickScore and CLIPScore point estimates at 768px, while 1024px parity is reported only for PickScore. OCR/NED for text rendering is inherited from the original quantization recipe and is not remeasured for the fused build. The same kernel loses on A100 and B200, where bf16 and FP8 paths are faster.25:T660,The method has three main components. First, the benchmark reformulation. Starting from SWE-Bench Verified, the full issue text is no longer given to the agent upfront. Instead, each task begins with a fixed initial user query that only partially exposes the problem. The agent keeps its normal programming actions but gains an additional message_user action, allowing it to ask follow-up questions and receive replies. This changes the task from pure code repair to joint specification elicitation plus code repair.

Second, the user simulator. Rather than using an unconstrained chat model, the paper builds a persona-grounded simulator whose responses are anchored to the full issue text. The design goal is twofold: preserve faithfulness to the original task specification, and produce realistic user-style interaction without leaking omniscient information. This is an important benchmarking choice. A free-form simulator would be cheaper to build but much noisier and less reproducible; a tightly grounded simulator makes automatic evaluation more stable.

Third, the schema-guided agent. The proposed agent is informed by dialogue theory and uses an explicit interaction schema to structure user communication, such as clarification, assumption checking, progress reporting, and decision requests. This is more than adding a prompt like “ask the user when needed.” The point is to make dialogue a structured policy component rather than incidental text generation. In many off-the-shelf coding agents, dialogue is either underused or poorly timed; the schema is intended to make asking behavior purposeful and actionable.26:T5d0,The experiments support three main claims. First, benchmark results show that stronger coding models in the standard autonomous setting do not necessarily rank higher in the dialogue setting. That is the central empirical result: interactive competence is not a free byproduct of better code generation.

Second, the proposed schema-guided agent improves over strong baselines by 3 to 14 percent, according to the paper’s headline numbers. For repository-level SWE tasks, that is a meaningful gain because absolute solve rates are typically low and a few points often correspond to a substantial number of recovered tasks. The paper argues that the gain comes from better clarification and alignment, not just stronger patch synthesis.

Third, the paper augments task success with automatic dialogue-quality evaluation using an LLM judge. This helps separate failure modes: some agents code well but do not communicate, some communicate but ask low-value questions, and some over-query the user. That decomposition is useful because final patch accuracy alone cannot explain why an agent failed.

From the provided full text, the benchmark is built by reformulating SWE-Bench Verified, the primary task metric remains resolved percentage via unit tests, and dialogue-level metrics are added on top. When reading the full paper, it is worth checking the exact task count, simulator settings, message frequency statistics, and the correlation between dialogue quality and task resolution.27:T4bb,Start with the introduction. The most important setup is why fully autonomous SWE evaluation is insufficient: many real issues are underspecified, and users frequently correct agents while agents rarely seek clarification. Then read the benchmark reformulation and simulator sections carefully, especially how the environment is modified and how the simulator is constrained to avoid information leakage and high variance.

If you only spend 20 to 30 minutes, focus on the task-flow figure, the simulator design, the main ranking table showing how agents reorder under dialogue evaluation, and the ablation or comparison for the schema-guided agent. If you have more time, read the dialogue-quality evaluation section, because that determines whether the benchmark measures meaningful interaction quality or merely adds a chat channel.

Three questions are worth probing. How much information is hidden in the initial query? If too little is hidden, dialogue becomes cosmetic; if too much is hidden, the task becomes guesswork. Does the simulator introduce a new overfitting target? And does the schema-guided gain come from genuinely better interaction structure or simply from longer, more cautious prompting?28:T6e5,The setup is clean and practical. Starting from the 419 pairwise instruction-following items in LLMBar, the authors create three versions of each item: original English (EN), full Chinese translation (ZH), and a natural Chinese-English language-switched version (LS) that preserves technical terms and named entities when appropriate. The critical requirement is label-preserving translation: the transformation must not silently fix factual, mathematical, formatting, or instruction-following errors in the original responses. To support this, the paper uses gpt-4.1-mini for transformation and adds an automatic audit for empty fields, severe length shrinkage, and large numeric-token changes, followed by a sensitivity analysis.

The evaluation protocol goes beyond standard accuracy. For each item and language condition, the judge sees both original and swapped answer order, so position bias is not conflated with language bias. Outputs A/B/Tie are normalized back to the original response identities. On top of that, the paper introduces translation-equivalence tie probes: the gold answer in English is compared against its Chinese translation, where the intended judgment is Tie. If a judge systematically prefers one language in this setting, that reveals language preference rather than quality sensitivity.

The metric suite is broader than typical judge evaluations. In addition to strict accuracy and tie-half accuracy, the paper reports language-invariance flip rate, gold-correctness flip rate, position inconsistency, source-level robustness, language preference, uncertainty estimates, paired significance tests, and usage cost. The useful distinction here is between being wrong and being unstable under label-preserving language transformations.29:T54b,The study evaluates four API-accessible judges and collects 13,408 successful judgments over the full 419-item benchmark. That is not a massive scale, but it is sufficient for a focused meta-evaluation because each item is expanded systematically across language conditions, answer-order swaps, and tie probes.

The experimental questions are well chosen. First, does standard accuracy remain stable across EN, ZH, and LS conditions? Second, how often does the same source item trigger a preference flip under language switching? Third, in the gold-answer translation tie probes, does the judge incorrectly prefer English or Chinese instead of Tie? Fourth, do the conclusions remain after excluding the 19 high-risk translated items flagged by the audit?

For this kind of paper, the most important result is not a single accuracy number but the error structure. A judge can have acceptable average accuracy while still being unreliable under language switching. That matters in practice. If a judge performs well in English but flips frequently in Chinese or mixed-language settings, it is a poor choice for multilingual evaluation. If it systematically prefers English in translation-equivalent probes, then ranking and reward signals may inherit that bias. The paper’s main contribution is turning that concern into a measurable diagnostic framework.2a:T4e0,This is a good skim paper; around 30 minutes is enough to extract the main value. Start with the introduction to understand that the target is not multilingual generation quality but judge reliability under language-preserving transformations. Then read the Judge-LS protocol and metrics sections carefully. Those two sections carry most of the substance.

If time is limited, focus on four things: how EN/ZH/LS variants are constructed and audited for label preservation; why answer-order swaps are necessary; how the translation-equivalence tie probes isolate language preference; and the main result tables showing flip rates and language preference across judges. The sensitivity analysis is also worth checking, because it tells you whether the conclusions are driven by a small number of questionable translations.

Two follow-up questions are worth keeping in mind. First, using gpt-4.1-mini as the transformation model may itself introduce stylistic regularities into the benchmark; the paper mitigates this mechanically, but semantic preservation is still not perfect. Second, English-Chinese is only the first test case. The protocol should extend naturally to code-mixed settings, lower-resource languages, and terminology-heavy domains.2b:T4ed,This is not a new model paper. It is a detailed expert-review case study. The authors used Claude Code, given a human-written theorem statement and a PDF proof sketch from Hartshorne, to semi-autonomously formalize Grothendieck’s vanishing theorem in Lean. The first fully compiling version is called state A. An expert Lean/mathlib review then identified substantial issues in definitions, theorem statements, API design, and file organization. Those review comments were converted into a checklist, and Claude plus Codex iterated until the checklist was addressed, producing state B.

The paper’s main contribution is the evaluation frame. It separates “kernel acceptance” from “library quality” and documents the difference with before/after expert review. One especially informative episode is the heartbeat debugging story. When typeclass search and proof checking became too expensive, the agent’s first move was to raise maxHeartbeats repeatedly. The durable fix was different: cache instances with inferInstanceAs, split large proofs into named lemmas, and restructure the development. That is a concrete example of the broader pattern: the agent follows locally executable fixes more readily than it recognizes structural design problems.2c:T43a,The evidence is a project-level reconstruction rather than a benchmark table. The development is around 5,000 lines of Lean code and targets a graduate-level algebraic geometry theorem, not a short contest-style proof. State A already reaches zero sorries and follows the intended mathematical proof structure, which is a strong sign that current models can handle substantial local proof completion when the route is fixed. The weaknesses appear under expert review: poor definitions, insufficient theorem generality, awkward namespace and file structure, and APIs that are hard to build on.

After the review-driven refactor, state B is substantially better as a library artifact. The paper does not reduce this to a single scalar metric. Instead it presents process evidence: the sorry curve, commit history, token usage, loop modes, and the evolution of expert-review findings. That is exactly the point. If one only measures “does it compile?”, state A already looks successful. If one measures “is this a good mathlib contribution?”, state A and state B are far apart.2d:T4f1,1. Under free dynamics, the critical grokking norm ||W||_c is highly concentrated, varying only 1-2% across a 2x range of training fractions and 10x range of learning rates. 2. Clamp experiments confirm causal control: networks generalize at every fixed norm in the 0.85-1.15||W||_c range, with no required hard threshold. Grokking time follows an exponential law T_grok ∝ e^{αρ} with R²>0.99 across this range; weight norm controls a ~19x span of grokking time, while learning rate acts as a secondary modulator with only ~2x effect. 3. Across 5 prime moduli, all tasks collapse to a shared exponent α≈7.5 when normalized by relative norm ρ (R²=0.996). The law enters sub-exponential saturation for ρ>1.25, with a minimum norm floor at the smallest task size. 4. The law replicates on unnormalized attention models with α≈15 (R²=0.999). LayerNorm decouples total weight norm from function scale, leaving only the functionally relevant unembedding norm concentrated and aligned with the law. 5. Sparse parity (a non-Fourier task) replicates the core finding that norm controls timing rather than acting as a hard threshold. 6. Fourier feature concentration rises in lockstep with norm relaxation, temporally aligning with circuit formation timelines.2e:T679,For each behavior, the authors build a matched contrastive dataset with positive examples that elicit the behavior and negative examples that suppress it. At a chosen layer of a decoder-only Transformer, they take the last-token residual-stream activation and compute paired contrast vectors between positive and negative examples. These vectors are centered and decomposed with SVD. Instead of representing a behavior by a single steering direction, the method represents it as a top-k low-rank subspace.

A key step is removing decision-aligned variance. The leading singular components can reflect the model’s final positive-vs-negative decision rather than behavior-specific structure. The paper defines a scalar decision margin using the log-probability difference of label strings, identifies components aligned with this margin, and separates them from the behavior subspace analysis. This is meant to avoid conflating decision readout with the internal representation of the behavior.

The diagnostic then studies two quantities. The first is geometric overlap between behavior subspaces, measured through projection-style or principal-angle-style comparisons. The second is decision coupling, which measures how strongly a behavior subspace is connected to the model’s decision direction. Projection-based interventions are applied to a source behavior subspace, and the induced changes are measured on target behaviors. Overlap explains whether an intervention can reach another behavior; decision coupling explains whether that shared representation actually affects the output. Their combination accounts for asymmetric intervention effects.2f:T5b6,The paper frames Adversarial Concept Search (ACS): given a set of concepts, extract a linear direction/encoding for each atomic concept from the model, then use pairwise geometry (primarily angular separation) to predict whether composing those concepts will succeed. The mechanism hypothesis is “lossy superposition causes interference”: while sparse feature activations can in principle allow lossless decoding even with non-orthogonal features, real models decode imperfectly; when multiple task-relevant features are simultaneously active and their directions are too aligned, they interfere and compositional generalization fails.
Operationally, the paper instantiates ‘atomic representations’ differently per task:
- In SCAN, atomic primitives are cleanly defined, enabling direct measurement of concept-pair interactions and angles.
- In multi-hop QA, a two-hop question is decomposed into two single-hop components; the angle between the two single-hop representations predicts whether the composed two-hop query will be answered correctly.
- In multilingual factual recall, geometry between fact representations and language subspaces/directions is used to predict recall accuracy for fact×language combinations.
The key distinction from standard error prediction/active learning is that ACS aims to work without running the model on candidate composed inputs; it tries to rank failure-prone scenarios “upstream” using concept-level probes.30:T795,The authors extend language generation in the limit to a nested-language setting. The universe is a countable set of strings U. An instance is a pair H⊆F⊆U, both infinite. F is the ambient formal language, accessible through a membership oracle; H is the hidden valuable language. At round t, the generator observes the current literature prefix S_t, may query the oracle for F, and must output a fresh string not already in S_t. Outputs fall into three categories: valuable, trivial, or hallucinated. Performance is measured in the limit by coverage of H, using lower-density style notions, rather than finite-horizon accuracy.

The first main result, Theorem 3.1, is the key conceptual one: for exact or approximate breadth, the oracle does not improve what families are learnably generable beyond the oracle-free case. The characterization remains fiber-wise and is governed by Angluin-style tell-tale conditions. In plain terms, the verifier can identify the ambient formal shell F, but it cannot distinguish among candidate valuable sublanguages H that live inside the same F. Taste must come from examples, not from the checker.

The second main result, Theorem 3.2, shows what the verifier does buy: sound coverage. The oracle can eliminate hallucinations by ensuring outputs stay inside F while still pursuing coverage of unseen valuable statements. But this only removes one failure mode. It does not solve triviality.

The later results establish the paper’s strongest impossibility claim: any generator that achieves broad coverage of unseen valuable mathematics must emit infinitely many trivial statements. The trivia rate can become asymptotically negligible, but the total amount cannot be finite. That is the “flood and harvest” trade-off in the title. Valuable unrecorded mathematics is reachable only through an exploration process that necessarily produces an unbounded stream of certified but worthless outputs.31:T455,This is a theory paper, so there are no model-scale experiments, training runs, or benchmark tables. The empirical role is played by constructions and formal separations. The motivating example in the introduction is useful: the authors show how one can cheaply generate a machine-verified statement decorated with famous mathematical objects while its actual content is still just 1+1=2. That example is not a theorem result by itself, but it captures the gap the theory formalizes.

The main takeaways from the results are threefold. First, the oracle does not help with value identification for breadth-style goals beyond identifying F. Second, it does help with soundness by ruling out hallucinations. Third, broad discovery of unseen valuable statements necessarily comes with infinitely many trivial outputs. What can be improved is the asymptotic rate of trivia, not its total elimination. For data synthesis and self-improvement pipelines, that reads like a lower bound: if the target notion of quality is not directly expressible by the verifier, low-value but valid samples are a structural outcome.32:T765,The method has three parts. First, the authors localize where “reading order” lives in the network. They use six-panel comic strips as a controlled environment because spatial layout naturally encodes narrative order. For the same strip, they compare a normal prompt with a prompt that asks the model to read the comic in reverse. From the residual stream before the answer, they compute a difference-of-means direction for each layer. They then inject that direction into a fresh forward pass and measure whether the answer flips from left-to-right reading to right-to-left reading. Only a narrow band of layers, roughly 20 to 28, produces the flip, indicating that ordering behavior is localized in mid-late layers.

Second, within that band they identify gaze heads using a simple correlation score. A head qualifies if its attention consistently lands on the panel currently being described and shifts as the narration moves forward. In Qwen3-VL-8B, about 100 out of 1,152 heads satisfy this pattern, or 8.7% of all heads. These heads cluster in the same mid-late region. The same discovery procedure recovers similar head sets across model sizes from 2B to 32B and across other VLM architectures, which argues against this being an artifact of one checkpoint.

Third, they perform causal interventions by directly modifying the attention masks of those heads at inference time. Redirecting the gaze heads to a chosen panel causes the model to describe that panel instead. The intervention is sharply tuned: too few redirected heads give only partial control, while too many break fluent generation by overriding heads needed for language modeling. They also show mid-generation steering, where the model finishes the current panel and then switches to the newly targeted one, suggesting that these heads participate in an online grounding mechanism rather than a one-shot global plan.33:T4aa,The main model is Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, with a 36-layer language backbone and 32 attention heads per layer, for 1,152 heads total. Discovery uses 3,948 comic samples from COMICS, converted into six-panel horizontal strips. Evaluation uses a disjoint set of 500 six-panel strips generated with GPT Image-1, where each panel is visually distinct enough to make grounding unambiguous.

Several results stand out. First, the reverse-reading direction only works when injected into layers 20–28, showing strong localization of reading-order control. Second, the authors identify a top-100 set of gaze heads, fewer than 9% of all heads. Third, redirecting only these heads steers the VQA answer to a chosen panel with 83.1% accuracy, versus a 16.7% chance level for six panels; comparable interventions on random non-gaze heads fail to redirect reliably. Fourth, the control effect is not monotonic: too little intervention is weak, too much damages generation quality. That supports the view that gaze heads form a functional subcircuit rather than a generic attention mass. Finally, the phenomenon transfers beyond comics to natural images and appears across multiple VLM sizes and architectures.34:T407,On Gemma-2-2B layer 12, adding a rank-24 bottleneck reduces the number of dense latents by up to 84% and improves sparse probing and targeted probe perturbation metrics at matched sparsity for both BatchTopK and Matryoshka variants.

The causal interventions are the most informative results. Removing the absorbed dense component increases cross-entropy by 7.5x, while removing the geometrically similar top-24 PCA directions increases it by 2.8x. This suggests that the bottleneck captures a computationally specific component rather than merely reproducing PCA. Sparse dictionaries appear to encode this content redundantly: ablating 787 maximally aligned sparse features raises cross-entropy by only 2.9x. In a topic-classification probe, ablating 2,048 topic-aligned sparse features leaves MMLU topic classification almost unchanged, whereas removing the dense component drops performance from 98.7% to chance. Post-hoc analysis links the absorbed component to top principal components and Bondarenko-style outlier dimensions.35:T4d7,The evaluation compares five memory substrates: none, markdown, vector, graph, and git. It covers GPQA-Diamond and MATH-500, two transfer regimes, two backbones, and sample sizes up to n=500. The memory hypothesis is rejected for novel problems: no substrate reliably improves accuracy. An early git trend of about +15 percentage points at n=40 failed a pre-registered replication. A similarity sweep explains the null result. Memory helps sharply only when the retrieved case is a near-duplicate of the test problem, around cosine similarity above 0.8, with gains of roughly +12 to +13.5 points. With a roughly 4.5x stronger backbone, the near-duplicate gain increases to about +22.5 to +28.5 points, while the method-transfer region remains null. Accuracy is moved more reliably by test-time sampling and model strength; self-consistency gives about +3.4 points at n=500. The paper’s GPQA headline result, 47.0% versus 33.0%, is attributed to MCQ-aware expansion and a larger compute budget, not to memory. On the substrate side, git preserves accuracy parity with other stores and adds about 15 ms per write. A merge demonstration shows that contradictory lessons surface as git merge conflicts instead of being silently concatenated.36:T4aa,RePro uses a forward-then-reflect rollout to generate two progress traces for the same episode.
- Forward pass: the agent interacts with the environment and emits an online progress estimate at each step along with the action.
- Reflect pass: after the episode ends and the final outcome is known, the model revisits the full trajectory and re-estimates step-wise progress retrospectively, producing a hindsight progress sequence that is assumed to be more reliable.
Training has two stages:
1) Retrospection Warmup: a small set of external-LLM demonstrations teaches the model the reflection format and basic behavior.
2) RePro-PO: RL with a composite reward that includes (a) retrospective progress shaping to provide dense per-step signals, (b) online–retrospective alignment to make online progress match the hindsight trace (reducing online noise), and (c) format regularization to prevent degenerate outputs / reward hacking.
Key distinction from standard approaches: instead of adding a separate critic/value model for credit assignment, RePro makes “progress” a policy-emitted latent/auxiliary variable, calibrates it with hindsight, and feeds it back as step-wise reward shaping.37:T4ae,HarnessX is a unified harness foundry with three core modules: 1) Composable harness: The harness is formalized as a first-class typed object, assembled from typed primitives (processors spanning context, tools, skills, control, memory) via a substitution algebra inserted at lifecycle hook points. A 9-dimensional taxonomy covers the full behavioral space, enabling type-safe component addition/removal and full decoupling. 2) AEGIS adaptive evolution engine: Harness adaptation is framed as a learning problem over symbolic artifacts (prompts, tools, memory, control policies). A four-stage pipeline (Digester for trace compression, Planner for adaptation planning, Evolver for candidate generation, Critic for assessment) combined with full trace observability addresses RL-aligned risks including reward hacking, catastrophic forgetting, and under-exploration. An optional variant-isolation strategy eliminates cross-task interference on heterogeneous benchmarks. 3) Harness-model co-evolution: A shared replay buffer enables interleaved harness evolution and model GRPO training, allowing the model to internalize strategies from successive harness versions to form a closed optimization but risks model collapse, where repeated training erodes distributional tails and homogenizes outputs. Data selection is widely viewed as a remedy, yet its reliability depends critically on the reference distribution used by the verifier. We show that in low-resource verification regimes, where each verifier observes only a small, fragmented, and biased slice of the target manifold, selection itself becomes bia","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"must_read","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-06-11T11:49:39Z","category_id":"pretraining_data","cover_url":"/covers/2606_13732.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.AI"],"summary":{"core_problem":"这篇论文研究的不是一般意义上的“合成数据会导致模型坍塌”，而是更具体也更现实的问题：当递归训练依赖数据选择来过滤合成样本时，如果验证器只能看到局部、稀缺、带偏的数据切片，那么“选择”本身会变成新的偏置来源。论文把场景放在数据孤岛和低资源机构上，例如医院、银行、联盟式协作环境。这里各方都不能共享原始数据，只能用本地参考分布去打分和筛选模型生成样本。问题在于，本地参考分布并不覆盖全局流形，结果是筛选机制会系统性保留贴近本地模式的样本，持续丢弃尾部和跨域模式。这样一来，原本被视为缓解 model collapse 的 sample selection，反而会加速多样性收缩，并把递归训练推向更强的分布偏移。","importance":"对预训练研究的意义主要有三层。第一，它把“数据选择能否拯救递归合成训练”这个问题从理想 oracle 设定拉回到真实训练基础设施约束下，指出选择器的参考分布质量本身就是一阶变量。很多关于 synthetic data 的讨论默认 verifier 足够接近真实分布，这篇工作说明在低资源和分布碎片化条件下，这个前提并不成立。第二，它把 model collapse 的成因从“生成器误差累积”扩展到“选择器偏差累积”，这对合成数据用于 continual pretrain、domain adaptation、联盟式训练都很关键。第三，它给出了一条不交换原始数据的缓解路径：用 Wasserstein 几何构造跨 silo 的代理参考分布。这个贡献不是简单工程补丁，而是在训练数据治理层面提出了一个可分析、可协作的机制。量级上，它更像是对 synthetic pretraining pipeline 的风险建模与修正框架，而不是单点提分方法。","technical_approach":"技术上，论文先把递归训练分成 replace、accumulate 和 selection 三类范式，并把分析重点放在 selection。前两类是已有文献常见设定：replace 只用新一轮合成数据，已知会快速坍塌；accumulate 把历史真实数据持续保留，能避免方差发散，但在很多低资源场景里真实数据规模太小，后续仍会被大量合成数据主导。论文关心的是更常见也更被寄予希望的 selection：每一轮先生成候选合成样本，再用 verifier 选出“高质量”子集用于下一轮训练。\n\n核心创新在于把 verifier 的参考分布显式建模为局部流形上的偏置观测，而不是全局真分布的近似。理论部分给出的直觉是：如果选择分数由某个 silo 的局部参考集决定，那么样本被保留的概率会随其与该局部流形的接近程度上升。这样，selection operator 不再是去噪器，而是一个带方向性的投影，会反复压缩全局分布的支撑集。论文进一步说明，这种压缩不是偶然现象，而会在递归迭代中以幂律速度导致多样性衰减和 Wasserstein 距离恶化。也就是说，selection bias 会被训练闭环放大。\n\n为缓解这个问题，作者提出协作式 verifier 框架。关键约束是不共享原始数据，因此不能直接做 pooled verification。论文利用 Wasserstein geometry 在各 silo 的经验分布之间构造代理参考：一种是 geodesic interpolation，即在两个或多个局部分布之间沿最优传输路径做插值；另一种是 Wasserstein barycenter，即求多个局部分布的几何中心。然后用这些代理分布替代单一 silo 的本地参考，对合成样本进行联合打分或筛选。这样做的理由很明确：如果单个 verifier 只能看到局部切片，那么就需要一个不暴露原始样本、但能在分布层面整合多方信息的参考对象。与直接平均特征、参数平均或简单投票不同，Wasserstein 代理保留了分布几何结构，尤其适合分析尾部覆盖和模式迁移。论文还讨论了理论上若 verifier 能访问全局 ground truth，selection 可以恢复为有效稳定器，但这在目标场景下不可行，因此协作代理是现实替代方案。","experiments":"从全文可见，实验的目标不是追求通用 benchmark 上的绝对分数，而是验证三件事：局部选择偏置是否会系统性诱发 collapse；这种退化是否会随递归轮数累积；协作式代理参考能否缓解。实验部分重点看 Section 6。作者构造了低资源、多 silo 的递归训练环境，对比 no selection、local selection、global-reference selection，以及基于 geodesic / barycenter 的 collaborative selection。\n\n关键观察有几类。第一，local selection 虽然短期内能提高样本“看起来像本地数据”的一致性，但会更快损失全局多样性，表现为分布方差收缩、尾部模式消失、与真实全局分布的 Wasserstein 距离增大。第二，这种退化不是一次性的，而是随着 generation 递推持续放大，符合理论部分给出的 asymptotic power-law collapse。第三，若用全局参考做选择，collapse 明显减轻，说明问题不在 selection 这个动作本身，而在 reference distribution 的覆盖不足。第四，在不能共享原始数据的前提下，Wasserstein geodesic 和 barycenter 代理都能显著优于单 silo 选择，尤其在 silo 间分布异质性较强时更明显。\n\n摘要里没有给出完整数字，提供的全文片段也未包含全部表格，因此这里不虚构具体百分点或样本规模。精读时应直接核对 Section 6 的主表、递归轮次曲线、Wasserstein 距离与方差变化图，以及不同 silo 数量、资源规模、异质性强度下的消融。真正有价值的不是单个终点指标，而是 collapse trajectory 的形状变化：local verifier 往往在早期看似稳定，后期却更快进入模式收缩；协作代理则主要改善尾部保留和长期稳定性。","reading_guide":"建议按“问题设定—理论机制—缓解方法—实验验证”四步读。\n\n第一步看 Introduction 和 Preliminaries。重点不是背景铺陈，而是作者如何重新定义问题：selection 不是天然安全，安全性取决于 verifier 的参考分布。这里要把 replace、accumulate、selection 三种递归范式区分清楚，因为后面的理论结论都建立在这个划分上。\n\n第二步精读 Section 3。这里是全文最关键的部分。要看清作者如何把 local prior 导致的 biased filter 形式化，以及为什么这种偏置会在递归训练中表现为 diversity contraction。尤其关注 Subsection 3.2 关于幂律收缩的推导和假设条件：哪些结论依赖低资源、哪些依赖分布碎片化、哪些依赖选择规则的单调性。\n\n第三步看 Section 4 的 Wasserstein 协作框架。重点不是公式推导本身，而是理解为什么选 geodesic interpolation 和 barycenter，而不是更简单的 pooled statistics 或 ensemble voting。这里值得深挖的是：代理分布到底在多大程度上恢复了全局流形；它缓解的是均值偏差、支撑集缺失，还是尾部质量不足。\n\n第四步看 Section 6 实验。优先看递归轮次上的动态曲线，而不是只看最终一轮。重点图表通常会展示 variance、Wasserstein distance、diversity coverage、不同 silo 异质性下的表现。若时间有限，先看 local vs collaborative vs global-reference 三组对比，再看 geodesic 与 barycenter 的差异。\n\n这篇论文值得深挖的点有两个。一个是它把 verifier 质量问题前置了，这对用 reward model、judge model、过滤器做合成数据清洗也有直接启发。另一个是它把分布协作放在数据层而不是参数层，这和联邦学习里常见的参数聚合是不同思路，适合继续追问：能否推广到 tokenizer 级别、语义嵌入级别，或更高维的文本生成分布。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The paper studies a more specific failure mode than the usual “synthetic data causes model collapse” story. Its target is recursive training with sample selection, under the realistic condition that the verifier only has access to a small, biased, local slice of the target distribution. This happens naturally in data silos such as hospitals, banks, or privacy-constrained consortia, where raw data cannot be pooled. In that setting, selection is no longer an unbiased quality filter. It becomes a local-prior amplifier: synthetic samples that align with the silo’s own manifold are retained, while globally valid but locally rare modes are discarded. Repeating this loop shrinks diversity, erodes tails, and pushes the model toward collapse even when selection was intended as the safeguard.","importance":"Its value for pretraining research is threefold. First, it challenges the common assumption that sample selection can stabilize recursive synthetic training as long as one filters aggressively enough. The paper shows that the reference distribution behind the verifier is itself a first-order variable. Second, it extends the collapse discussion from generator error accumulation to selector bias accumulation. That matters for synthetic pretraining, continual pretraining, domain adaptation, and federated or siloed training pipelines. Third, it proposes a concrete mitigation path that does not require raw-data sharing: constructing collaborative proxy references with Wasserstein geometry. This is less about squeezing out benchmark gains and more about correcting a structural weakness in synthetic-data training loops.","technical_approach":"$16","experiments":"$17","reading_guide":"$18","authors_note":""},"recommendation":{"zh":"把“数据筛选能防崩塌”这个直觉打了个洞：参考分布不全时筛选会系统性杀尾部；建议复现其toy设定检验自家过滤管线。","en":"Pokes a real hole in the “filtering prevents collapse” intuition: with incomplete reference distributions, selection kills tails systematically—worth reproducing the toy regime against your filtering pipeline."},"metadata":{"institutions":[],"authors":["Xinbao Qiao","Xianglong Du","Wei Liu","Jingqi Zhang","Peihua Mai","Meng Zhang","Yan Pang"],"notable_authors":[],"tags":["model-collapse","synthetic-data","data-selection","distribution-shift","tail-modes"],"links":{"arxiv":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13732","pdf":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.13732","github":null,"project":null,"source_url":null,"article":null}}},{"id":"2606.13808","title":"The Culture Funnel: You Can't Align What isn't in the Data","abstract":"Current cultural alignment approaches focus on inference-time interventions, assuming models already contain sufficient cultural knowledge. We argue modern LLM pipelines suffer from a cultural data funnel. Using a multidimensional tagging framework across pretraining, fine-tuning, alignment, and reasoning datasets, we show explicit cultural signals decline sharply during post-training, while geographically concentrated, task-specialized data dominates. Multilinguality enhances geographic diversi","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"skim","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-06-11T18:21:10Z","category_id":"pretraining_data","cover_url":"/covers/2606_13808.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.CL"],"summary":{"core_problem":"这项工作研究文化对齐失败是否来自训练数据管线，而不是只来自推理时提示或对齐方法。核心观点是：预训练语料中可能存在一定文化信号，但经过 SFT、偏好对齐、推理数据和任务化数据筛选后，显式文化信息会被压缩，形成所谓 culture funnel。","importance":"对预训练数据研究的启发比较明确。它把文化对齐从“模型有没有文化知识”转成“训练阶段是否给了模型足够、均衡、可学习的文化信号”。这对 data mixture、post-training 数据配比、合成推理数据污染都有意义。现在很多后训练数据强调数学、代码、推理和通用帮助性，容易把地域、生活方式、社会规范、价值偏好等信号稀释掉。多语数据可以增加地域覆盖，但不能自动保证文化覆盖均衡，这一点对 multilingual pretraining 和 instruction tuning 都有参考价值。","technical_approach":"工作构建了一个自动文化标注管线，对 5.6M 条样本做多维标签。标签维度包括文化类型、领域、任务意图、地理位置和语言。文化标签采用面向 NLP 的 taxonomy，包含 Culture as Knowledge、Dynamics、Preference、Bias，并额外区分 General Culture 和 No Culture。语言识别使用 FastText LangID，其余标注主要使用 Command-A。预训练语料按整段文本标注；后训练和对话数据只标注用户侧 prompt 或 instruction，因为这些部分决定了模型是否有机会学习文化语境。数据选择覆盖预训练、后训练、评测和真实用户对话，包括 CulturaX、Dolci Instruct-SFT、GeoFact-X、CultureBank、MultiNRC、Aya 原创标注、PRISM 和 ShareLM 等。","experiments":"实验结果显示，显式文化信号在训练管线后段明显下降，后训练数据更集中在少数地理区域和任务类型上。多语言数据提升了地理多样性，但语言不是文化的充分代理，仍会出现覆盖不均。工作还报告了一个实用结果：加入显式文化标签后，下游文化 benchmark 表现提升，说明文化标记不只是分析工具，也可以作为训练或数据筛选信号。需要注意的是，标注依赖自动模型，结论更适合作为大规模数据分布诊断，而不是对单条样本文化充分性的人工判定。","reading_guide":"泛读建议 25 到 35 分钟。先看数据选择和 tagging taxonomy，确认它如何定义文化、地理位置、任务意图和语言。然后看各训练阶段的文化信号变化图表，这是主要结论来源。最后看文化标签用于下游 benchmark 的实验，判断这些标签是否能转化为训练收益。对做数据配比的人，重点不是具体 benchmark 分数，而是它提供了一种审计 post-training 数据的方式：检查指令数据、偏好数据和推理数据是否系统性稀释了文化语境。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The work asks whether failures in cultural alignment are caused by the training data pipeline rather than only by inference-time prompting or alignment methods. Its central claim is that cultural signals may exist in pretraining corpora, but are progressively compressed during SFT, alignment, reasoning-data construction, and task-specialized post-training.","importance":"The paper is most relevant as a pretraining and post-training data study. It reframes cultural alignment as a data-mixture and signal-preservation problem: models cannot reliably learn culturally situated behavior if the relevant contexts are sparse, geographically concentrated, or removed by later curation stages. This matters for multilingual pretraining, instruction tuning, synthetic reasoning data, and preference-data design. A useful point is that multilinguality improves geographic coverage but does not by itself provide balanced cultural representation.","technical_approach":"The authors build an automatic tagging pipeline and apply it to 5.6M samples across datasets from different stages of the LLM pipeline. Each sample is annotated along multiple dimensions: cultural content, domain, task intent, geolocation, and language. The cultural taxonomy includes Culture as Knowledge, Dynamics, Preference, and Bias, with additional labels for General Culture and No Culture. Language is tagged with FastText LangID, while the other annotations are produced mainly with Command-A. For pretraining corpora, the full text is tagged. For post-training and conversational data, only user-side prompts or instructions are tagged, because these determine whether the model is exposed to a culturally grounded learning opportunity. The dataset pool includes CulturaX, Dolci Instruct-SFT, GeoFact-X, CultureBank, MultiNRC, Aya original annotations, PRISM, and ShareLM.","experiments":"The main empirical finding is a sharp decline of explicit cultural signals in later training stages. Post-training datasets become more geographically concentrated and more dominated by task-specialized content such as reasoning, coding, and generic instruction following. Multilingual data increases geographic diversity but still leaves cultural representation imbalanced. The paper also reports that explicit cultural tags improve performance on downstream cultural benchmarks, suggesting that the tags can be used not only for auditing but also for data selection or training interventions. The main limitation is that the analysis relies on automatic tagging, so the results are best interpreted as large-scale distributional diagnostics rather than fine-grained human judgments of cultural adequacy.","reading_guide":"Skim time: 25–35 minutes. Then focus on the stage-wise distribution results that show how cultural signals change from pretraining to post-training. The downstream benchmark section is useful for checking whether the tags have training utility. For data researchers, the main value is the audit protocol: inspect whether instruction, preference, and reasoning datasets systematically remove culturally grounded contexts.","authors_note":""},"recommendation":{"zh":"读标注体系和各训练阶段的漏斗统计；动作是审数据配比，不是再加推理时补丁。","en":"Read the taxonomy and stage-wise funnel statistics; the action item is auditing data mixtures, not adding another inference-time patch."},"metadata":{"institutions":["Cohere Labs"],"authors":["Ananya Sahu","Mehrnaz Mofakhami","Daniel D'Souza","Thomas Euyang","Julia Kreutzer","Marzieh Fadaee"],"notable_authors":["Julia Kreutzer","Marzieh Fadaee"],"tags":["pretraining-data","cultural-alignment","data-mixture","multilinguality","dataset-audit"],"links":{"arxiv":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13808","pdf":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.13808","github":null,"project":null,"source_url":null,"article":null}}}]},{"id":"llm_pretrain","emoji":"🧠","name":"LLM 预训练","description":"文本LLM预训练的训练侧：优化器、学习率日程、训练 recipe、训练稳定性、并行/分布式训练、重磅技术报告（数据为核心的工作归 pretraining_data；Scaling Law 归 scaling；不含 diffusion/flow/any-order 等建模范式）","papers":[{"id":"2606.13894","title":"Gefen: Optimized Stochastic Optimizer","abstract":"AdamW is a default optimizer for modern deep learning, but its first and second moment states add roughly two parameter-sized buffers to training memory. We propose Gefen, a memory-efficient optimizer that automatically shares second-moment estimates across parameter blocks and quantizes the first moment using a learned codebook, thereby reducing AdamW's memory footprint by ~8x while maintaining the same performance, corresponding to a reduction of 6.5 GiB per billion parameters. The method is m","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"must_read","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-06-11T20:38:09Z","category_id":"llm_pretrain","cover_url":"/covers/2606_13894.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.LG","cs.AI","cs.CL","cs.CV"],"summary":{"core_problem":"这篇工作处理的是一个很实际、也很卡大模型训练上限的问题：AdamW 的优化器状态太占显存。标准 AdamW 需要为每个参数额外维护一阶矩 m 和二阶矩 v，两者合起来大约是 2× 参数量级的额外内存。在百亿到千亿参数训练里，这部分开销会直接挤压 batch size、序列长度、激活重计算策略和并行切分空间。已有路线主要有两类：一类是像 Adam-mini 那样按人工规则共享二阶矩，另一类是对优化器状态做量化。但前者依赖架构先验和张量命名，泛化性差；后者通常用手工 codebook 或 block-wise 量化，存在额外超参和失真控制问题。Gefen 要解决的是：在不依赖人工分组规则的前提下，自动找到适合共享二阶矩的参数块，并进一步压缩一阶矩状态，把 AdamW 的状态内存显著降下来，同时尽量保持 AdamW 级别的收敛和最终质量。","importance":"对预训练研究的意义很直接，而且量级不小。它不是单纯做一个“更省内存的工程技巧”，而是在优化器设计里把三件事连起来了：二阶结构的理论解释、自动分组算法、以及可精确求解的一阶矩量化。结果是把 AdamW 的优化器状态内存压到约 1/8，对应每 1B 参数节省约 6.5 GiB。这种量级的节省，在大模型预训练里通常可以换成更大的全局 batch、更长上下文、更少的并行碎片，或者在同样硬件上训练更大的模型。更重要的是，它给“为什么可以共享二阶矩”补上了比经验规则更扎实的解释：高 Hessian 耦合的参数，其平方梯度比值会被约束到更接近 1，因此共享 v 不是纯经验近似，而是有局部曲率上的依据。对做训练 recipe、优化器状态压缩、甚至 ZeRO/分布式内存预算的人，这篇都值得认真看。","technical_approach":"方法分两部分。第一部分是二阶矩共享。作者先从一个两层 MLP 的理论分析出发，研究混合 Hessian 项较大时，不同参数的平方梯度何时会接近。结论是：在可行的 Hessian 范围内，混合 Hessian 的绝对值越大，两个参数平方梯度之比允许的区间越收缩到 1 附近。这给出了一个很清楚的设计信号：如果一组参数在 Hessian 上强耦合，那么它们共享 Adam 的二阶矩估计，失真会比较小。问题在于大模型里不能显式算 Hessian，所以 Gefen 不直接估 Hessian，而是用首步梯度 g0 的平方结构做代理。具体做法是：对每个参数张量先展平，对长度 n 的张量枚举其所有 proper divisors p，把 g0^2 reshape 成若干个连续 block，每个 block 大小为 p，然后计算块内异质性指标 E(p)。E(p) 小，说明这个分块下块内平方梯度更相似，适合共享一个二阶矩标量；p 大则共享更激进、更省内存。算法在“更大块、更低异质性”之间做权衡，自动选出分块周期。这里一个有意思的点是，E(p) 不一定随 p 单调变差；某些更大的 divisor 反而更贴合真实结构，比如 attention head 或卷积通道带来的块结构，所以自动搜索比手工按名字切块更稳。\n\n第二部分是一阶矩压缩。Gefen 不是简单做低比特均匀量化，而是复用前面得到的 block partition，对一阶矩 m 做基于可学习 codebook 的量化，并且量化分配通过动态规划精确求解。作者强调两点：一是 codebook 是学习得到的，不是手工固定；二是复用同一套 block 划分，因此不会再引入一个独立的 block-size 超参。整体上，Gefen 可以理解为：v 用共享标量做结构化压缩，m 用 codebook 做离散压缩，两者都围绕同一个自动发现的参数块结构展开。这样设计比单独压 v 或单独量化 m 更完整，也比 Adam-mini 这类依赖架构规则的方法更通用。","experiments":"从文中给出的主结果看，Gefen 的核心结论有三层。第一层是内存：相对 AdamW，优化器状态内存约缩小 8×，作者给出的换算是每 1B 参数节省约 6.5 GiB。这是最硬的结果。第二层是性能保持：在 LLM 和非 LLM 网络上，Gefen 都能维持与 AdamW 可比的训练质量，部分设置下还有小幅收益。引言里明确说它在多种模型和数据集上取得了最低的内存占用，同时质量和吞吐与对比方法相当或更好。第三层是自动分组的有效性：论文不仅给了两层 MLP 的理论，还在附录展示了真实网络张量——包括 attention tensor 和卷积层——中，按自动发现的块结构切分后，平方梯度在块内更一致，这支持了“用首步梯度结构近似 Hessian 耦合”的经验合理性。\n\n和基线的比较重点应当包括 AdamW、Adam-mini，以及量化优化器一类方法。Adam-mini 的问题是需要用户提供架构信息，例如 attention heads 数量，还依赖 tensor name；Gefen 则完全自动。量化优化器的常见问题是手工 codebook 和额外 block-size 超参；Gefen 用 learned codebook 和动态规划避免了这点。文中还强调吞吐没有因为自动分组而明显受损，因为分组只在第一步基于 g0 推断一次，而不是训练中持续重估。精读时建议重点核对实验表里不同模型规模、不同任务上的最终 loss / accuracy / perplexity 差异，以及消融里“只共享 v”“只量化 m”“自动分组 vs 手工分组”的对比，这些最能看出方法是否只是内存换质量，还是确实找到了较优折中。","reading_guide":"建议按“理论—算法—实验”三段读。\n\n先看理论分析部分，重点抓住那个核心命题：混合 Hessian 大，平方梯度比值会被压向 1。这里不用过度纠缠证明细节，但要看清假设条件、适用对象，以及作者为什么说这个现象不仅出现在两层 MLP，也能在真实网络张量里观察到。这个部分决定了方法不是拍脑袋做 block sharing。\n\n再看算法部分。需要重点理解三个点：第一，为什么只用第一步梯度 g0 就足够推断共享结构；第二，E(p) 这个异质性指标如何定义、如何在内存和失真之间选 p；第三，一阶矩量化为什么要和同一套 block partition 绑定，以及动态规划在这里精确优化的是什么。如果你关心工程落地，这一节最值得细抠。\n\n实验部分建议优先看主表、内存统计表、吞吐对比和消融。主问题不是“有没有赢一点点指标”，而是“在 8× 状态压缩下，质量掉了多少、吞吐变了多少、适用范围有多广”。如果这些表里 Gefen 在 LLM 预训练设置下和 AdamW 基本打平，而明显优于 Adam-mini 或通用量化优化器，那它的实用价值就成立了。附录里 attention / conv 张量的平方梯度可视化也值得看，这些图能帮助判断自动分组到底是在捕捉真实结构，还是只是偶然拟合首步噪声。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The paper targets a concrete bottleneck in large-scale training: AdamW’s optimizer states are expensive. Standard AdamW stores both first and second moments, so optimizer memory is roughly 2× parameter memory on top of the weights themselves. At LLM scale, this directly constrains batch size, context length, activation checkpointing choices, and parallelization strategy. Existing memory-saving alternatives are unsatisfactory in different ways. Manual sharing schemes such as Adam-mini rely on architecture-specific heuristics and tensor-name conventions. Quantized optimizers reduce memory, but often depend on hand-crafted codebooks and introduce extra tuning knobs such as quantization block size. Gefen aims to remove these weaknesses at once: automatically discover which parameters can share second-moment statistics, compress first moments as well, and preserve AdamW-like optimization behavior without requiring model-specific rules.","importance":"This matters for pretraining in a very practical sense. The contribution is not just a lighter optimizer implementation; it connects theory, algorithm design, and systems impact. The reported reduction is about 8× in optimizer-state memory, or roughly 6.5 GiB saved per 1B parameters. At current model scales, that is large enough to change feasible training regimes: larger global batch, longer sequences, fewer memory-driven compromises, or simply larger models on the same hardware budget. The more interesting part is conceptual. The paper gives a principled reason for sharing second moments: if two parameters have strong mixed-Hessian coupling, their squared gradients are constrained to be similar, so sharing v is not merely a heuristic. For researchers working on optimizer design, memory-efficient pretraining, or distributed training budgets, this is a useful piece of work because it turns an empirical trick into a more grounded design pattern.","technical_approach":"$19","experiments":"$1a","reading_guide":"$1b","authors_note":""},"recommendation":{"zh":"应立刻看大模型 loss 曲线和 spike 统计；8x optimizer-state 压缩若稳定，训练配额会被重新分配。","en":"Check the large-model loss curves and spike statistics immediately; stable 8x optimizer-state compression would change training-memory allocation."},"metadata":{"institutions":[],"authors":["Nadav Benedek","Tomer Koren","Ohad Fried"],"notable_authors":[],"tags":["optimizer","adamw","training-memory","quantization","pretraining-systems"],"links":{"arxiv":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13894","pdf":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.13894","github":null,"project":null,"source_url":null,"article":null}}},{"id":"2606.13867","title":"Muon$^p$: Muon with Fractional Spectral Powers","abstract":"Muon is an increasingly widely used optimizer that replaces a gradient $G=USV^\\top$ with its polar factor $UV^\\top$, thereby flattening the singular spectrum. However, full flattening discards singular-value information that may matter for adaptation. We introduce Muon$^p$, a Muon-style optimizer that instead uses fractional spectral-power updates $US^pV^\\top$ for rational $p\\in(0,1)$, interpolating between Muon and gradient descent. To make it practical, we prove that fractional spectral powers","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"skim","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-06-11T19:48:12Z","category_id":"llm_pretrain","cover_url":"/covers/2606_13867.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.LG"],"summary":{"core_problem":"Muon 把梯度矩阵 G=USV^T 替换成极分解方向 UV^T，等价于把所有奇异值压平成 1。这个做法能平衡不同奇异方向的更新，但也会丢掉奇异值大小携带的适应性信息。Muon^p 试图解决的问题是：能否保留 Muon 的矩阵级谱归一化优势，同时不过度抹平梯度谱结构。","importance":"这项工作对预训练和微调优化器研究有直接参考价值。它把 Muon 从一个固定的极分解更新扩展成一族谱几何更新：US^pV^T，p 位于 0 到 1 之间。p=0 接近 Muon，p=1 接近普通梯度方向。这个连续插值使优化器可以按训练阶段选择不同的谱偏置。对大模型训练来说，关键启发不是“换一个优化器超参”，而是把矩阵梯度的奇异谱当作可调的学习率分配对象：强压平有利于均衡学习，保留部分谱信息有利于适应任务结构。作者也指出 Muon^p 更偏向微调收益，而不一定在所有预训练阶段优于 Muon，这个限制很重要。","technical_approach":"方法核心是用 fractional spectral power 更新替代 Muon 的 polar factor 更新。给定 G=USV^T，Muon 使用 UV^T；Muon^p 使用 US^pV^T，其中 p 是 0 到 1 之间的有理数。理论上，作者证明 US^pV^T 是 Schatten-q 范数约束下的最速下降方向，q=1+1/p。这给了更新规则一个几何解释：不同 p 对应不同的矩阵范数几何，也对应不同的谱方向重加权。\n\n实现难点在于不能直接做 SVD。Muon 的 UV^T 可以用 Newton-Schulz 这类只含矩阵乘法的迭代近似。作者证明，任意分数谱幂不能由固定的一元多项式迭代得到；原因是只对当前矩阵做一元多项式变换不足以同时保留原始奇异值与迭代状态。为此，作者构造低阶奇 bivariate recurrence，也就是同时使用原始矩阵和当前迭代量的双变量多项式递推。这样仍然只需要矩阵乘法，保留了 Muon 在加速硬件上的实现形态。工程上它接近 Muon 的一行替换，但数学上从 polar factor 扩展到任意 rational p。","experiments":"可见内容显示实验主要覆盖微调场景，而不是把 Muon^p 宣称为通用预训练替代。作者报告 Muon^p 在多种模型上提升数学推理、代码推理、验证困惑度和下游任务表现，并分析了它不如 Muon 的情形。分析维度包括谱学习率适应、课程阶段和 effective-rank 动态。给出的摘要和正文片段没有暴露具体表格数值、模型规模和提升幅度，因此不宜补写精确数字。更稳妥的结论是：Muon^p 的收益具有训练阶段依赖性，微调中保留部分奇异值信息更有帮助；在需要强谱均衡的阶段，完全压平的 Muon 仍可能更合适。","reading_guide":"建议泛读时间 30 到 45 分钟。优先看第 2 节的 Theorem 2.1、Lemma 2.2 和 Theorem 2.3。Theorem 2.1 说明为什么 US^pV^T 不是随意插值，而是对应 Schatten 范数下的最速下降；Lemma 2.2 解释为什么不能直接复用 Muon 的一元多项式迭代；Theorem 2.3 是工程可用性的关键。实验部分重点看不同 p 的曲线、微调与预训练或早期训练的差异，以及 effective rank 分析。真正需要深挖的是 p 的选择是否可以随训练阶段自适应，而不是固定成一个新的默认值。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"Muon replaces a matrix gradient G=USV^T with its polar factor UV^T, which flattens the singular spectrum completely. This can improve balance across singular directions, but it also removes magnitude information that may be useful for adaptation. Muon^p asks whether one can keep the matrix-level spectral benefits of Muon while retaining part of the singular-value structure.","importance":"The work is relevant to optimizer design for large-scale training and finetuning. It turns Muon from a single polar-factor update into a family of spectral updates, US^pV^T with p in (0,1). The endpoints connect Muon-like full flattening and gradient-descent-like magnitude preservation. The useful idea for pretraining research is that singular directions can receive a controllable form of matrix-level learning-rate allocation. Full flattening is not always the right bias; the preferred spectral geometry depends on the training regime. The paper is careful in this respect: the reported gains are strongest in finetuning, while Muon can still be preferable in regimes where aggressive spectral balancing is useful.","technical_approach":"The optimizer update is defined as US^pV^T for a gradient matrix G=USV^T. The paper gives this rule a norm-geometric interpretation: for q=1+1/p, US^pV^T is the steepest-descent direction under a Schatten-q norm constraint. This connects the choice of p to an explicit matrix norm rather than treating it as a heuristic interpolation.\n\nThe implementation problem is the main technical point. Computing US^pV^T by SVD is too expensive. Muon avoids SVD for UV^T using Newton-Schulz-style matrix-multiplication iterations. The paper proves that arbitrary fractional spectral powers cannot be computed by a fixed univariate polynomial iteration. A recurrence must retain access to the original matrix as well as the current iterate. The proposed solution is a low-degree odd bivariate polynomial recurrence that approximates US^pV^T using only matrix multiplications. This keeps the hardware profile close to Muon and makes the change small at the implementation level.","experiments":"The visible content indicates that the empirical section focuses on finetuning. Muon^p is reported to improve math reasoning, code reasoning, validation perplexity, and downstream task metrics across several models. The paper also studies failure or non-beneficial cases through spectral learning-rate adaptation, curriculum effects, and effective-rank dynamics. Exact model sizes, table values, and improvement margins are not exposed in the provided text, so precise numbers should not be inferred. The safe empirical reading is that fractional spectral powers are most useful when preserving some gradient-spectrum information helps task adaptation, while standard Muon may remain better when stronger spectrum flattening is needed.","reading_guide":"A 30-45 minute skim is enough for most readers. Focus on Theorem 2.1 for the Schatten-norm interpretation, Lemma 2.2 for the impossibility of a fixed univariate iteration, and Theorem 2.3 for the practical bivariate recurrence. In the experiments, look for the sensitivity to p, the split between finetuning and other regimes, and the effective-rank analysis. The most interesting follow-up question is whether p should be scheduled or adapted during training rather than chosen as a fixed optimizer setting.","authors_note":""},"recommendation":{"zh":"Muon 是否压谱过头是干净问题；只看算法递推、Schatten 解释和 billion-scale 消融。","en":"The clean question is whether Muon over-flattens the spectrum; read the recurrence, Schatten-norm interpretation, and billion-scale ablations."},"metadata":{"institutions":[],"authors":["Yihe Dong","Will Sawin"],"notable_authors":[],"tags":["optimizer","muon","spectral-methods","training-dynamics"],"links":{"arxiv":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13867","pdf":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.13867","github":null,"project":null,"source_url":null,"article":null}}},{"id":"2606.14560","title":"Free Heavy-Tailed Lunch for Muon: A Theoretical Justification of Empirical Success","abstract":"Non-Euclidean optimisation methods with matrix-valued updates, such as Muon and Scion, have recently shown strong empirical performance for training Transformer models, yet their theoretical advantages over Euclidean methods remain poorly understood. We address this gap in the heavy-tailed non-convex regime, where stochastic gradients have bounded $p$-th central moments, $p \\in (1,2]$. We show that certain non-Euclidean methods achieve optimal sample complexity under stronger stationarity measur","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"skim","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-06-12T15:37:36Z","category_id":"llm_pretrain","cover_url":"/covers/2606_14560.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["math.OC","cs.LG","stat.ML"],"summary":{"core_problem":"这篇工作要回答的问题很具体：为什么 Muon 这类基于非欧几里得几何、矩阵值更新的优化器，在训练 Transformer 时经常比标准欧式方法更稳、更快，但现有理论却解释不清。已有分析通常依赖有界方差噪声和欧式范数等价，把非欧几里得方法的噪声项重新压回 Euclidean geometry 里处理，结果会引入明显的维度因子，尤其对 Muon 的核范数平稳性分析会带来与 min{m,n} 相关的额外代价。论文把问题放到更贴近大模型训练现实的 heavy-tailed stochastic gradient 场景：只假设随机梯度在原生对偶几何下具有有界 p 阶中心矩，p∈(1,2]。核心目标是证明，非欧几里得归一化最速下降方法不仅能在更强的平稳性指标下收敛，而且在重尾噪声下相对欧式方法确实存在严格的复杂度优势；对 Muon，还要说明这种优势不是经验偶然，而是由核范数几何本身带来的。","importance":"对预训练研究的意义主要有三层。第一，它给 Muon 这类近期在 Transformer 训练中被大规模采用的优化器补上了理论缺口，而且不是一般性的收敛性结论，而是直接解释了“为什么在矩阵参数、重尾梯度、强平稳性指标下会更合适”。这比单纯证明能收敛更有用。第二，论文强调应在优化器的原生几何里建模噪声，而不是先退回欧式空间再分析；这个视角对大模型训练很关键，因为 embedding、attention、MLP 权重天然是矩阵，梯度噪声也常表现出重尾。第三，它给出 Muon 在 Schatten-1 平稳性下几乎最优的样本复杂度，并配套 lower bound，说明想在同样强的 stationarity measure 下做到完全 dimension-free 基本不可能。这对优化器选择有直接启发：如果关心矩阵结构和谱几何，非欧式方法不是“换个更新公式”，而是在目标几何和噪声模型匹配时能减少不必要的维度惩罚。量级上，这不是训练 recipe
